§1 — The goal, in the lead's terms

A playable Grad-Shafranov forward solve in Nova: a person moves the plasma about with keyboard controls and watches the flux map update in a Bokeh view at human response time, ignoring true dynamics for now. The controls act on the separatrix directly, through low-degree-of-freedom constraints that move its turning points and its position rather than through a rigid profile target. The forward solve carries the coils and the first wall, so what the person steers is a physical equilibrium under the machine's own actuators. Achieving this enables the next step: training a MAST video decoder that maps forward actions to a synthetic plasma recording, the first step towards a fusion world model backed by physics. The decoder and the world model belong to imas-ambix; this plan delivers the backbone they consume.

§2 — Controls: the pulse-design constraint set on the forward solve

The specification of a flux value and a field gradient at a few points both bounds the separatrix in its R-Z box and sets its shape within it. The inverse design in nova/equilibrium/inverse.py already constrains the boundary flux at the four bounding-box control points (eight with squareness, two strike points when diverted), zero radial field at the outer and inner points (a vertical field line, hence a radial turning point on the low-field and high-field sides), zero vertical field at the upper and lower points, and the null condition at the X-point, with the points generated by ControlPoints in nova/geometry/plasmapoints.py from geometric axis, minor radius, elongation, triangularities and squareness. The transfer of this set onto the forward solve's constraint interface, with compensating circuit directions read from the response matrix, is constraint-augmented-newton-krylov §4 (its followup on the bounding-box rows). This plan consumes those rows as the control vocabulary: bulk R and Z through the box, elongation and triangularity through the turning points, gaps through the wall-gap row, X-point location through the null row. A keyboard action is a change of one of those parameters; the solver re-solves from the previous equilibrium as a warm start.

§3 — Performance: a keyframe budget on the human scale

Human response time gives a budget of order 100 ms per keyframe. The reduced-state plain-Newton route (millisecond-converged-solve §2) measures an accepting Newton step at 2.15 ms and a warm trip at 46 ms on the four MAST bank rows, with 95 percent of a trip being one topology read; a keyframe that warm-starts from the previous equilibrium needs one to three trips, so the budget is reachable once the topology read at the trip boundary is cut and the constrained solve runs on the reduced route. Required here: the constraint rows augmenting the reduced Newton solve rather than only the Newton–Krylov ladder, a warm-start contract (a moved control point re-solved from the previous state converges in fewer trips than from the seed), and a measured keyframe wall on the H200 with the flux rendered, so the interactive budget is a receipt rather than a projection.

§4 — Interface: keyboard controls and a Bokeh view

The Bokeh application under apps/pulsedesign/ already renders a plasma, its control points, X-points and the wall from the inverse design with sliders for minor radius, gap, elongation and the triangularities. The playable solve re-uses that view over the forward solve: a server-side loop holds the current equilibrium, maps key presses to control-point moves (bulk R and Z, elongation, upper and lower triangularity, X-point, gaps), re-solves, and pushes the flux map, the separatrix, the control points, the compensating currents and the keyframe wall to the page. Deliverables: the key map, the solve loop with its warm start, the flux-map render at the keyframe rate, and a receipt of keyframe wall and trips per action on one MAST equilibrium.

§5 — The decoder seam

What the decoder consumes is the sequence of forward states with their actions: the flux map on the lattice, the separatrix and turning points, the coil and compensating currents, and the action that produced each frame. Those cross the boundary to imas-ambix as data, not as code (the coupled-repository rule: ambix owns the corpus reading and the decoder). This plan records the frame schema it emits and the receipt that a recorded steering session round-trips through it; the decoder training itself is ambix work and is not scheduled here.

§6 — Serving: the solve on an H200 job, the controls and playback over the tunnel

The lead's direction of 2026-09-05 fixes the deployment shape: the playable solve runs on the H200 reservation when it is live, and a person at a local client steers it and watches it through the same kind of localhost-plus-ssh-tunnel path the reckon docs server already uses, with the tunnels managed in imas-codex. Three existing pieces compose it, none new. The reckon docs server is reckon serve --port 8765 bound to 127.0.0.1 on the login node under the user unit reckon.service (logs to a file, restart on failure), and the laptop reaches it as localhost:8765 through imas-codex tunnel start iter --docs, which batches every requested forward into one autossh connection per host, records the tunnel pid, and installs a persistent imas-codex-tunnel-iter user unit on the client. For a service on a compute node the same tunnel binds the remote end of the forward to the node itself: imas_codex/remote/tunnel.py discovers the node with squeue -n <service_job_name> --format=%N (discover_compute_node) and the vLLM target forwards 127.0.0.1:<port>:<node>:<port>, falling back to the last node recorded in an allocation.log when the scheduler is unreachable. The H200 node is 98dci4-gpu-0003 on partition betelgeuse, reservation gpu_0003_grpA, whose time limit is infinite, so a serving job asks for hours and is never truncated by a self-imposed cap.

The shape that follows. Server: one sbatch job named nova-playable on betelgeuse under the reservation, one GPU, running the Bokeh server of §4 from the checkout with the root environment's interpreter directly (no uv on the node), JAX_PLATFORMS=cuda,cpu, the persistent compilation cache configured, bound on the node's interfaces on a port in the imas-codex service band (proposed 18506), with the websocket origins the browser will present (localhost:18506 and the node's own name) allowed, the resident MAST operator and carrier loaded once at start, and an allocation record (job id, node, port, start time, nova revision) written where the tunnel's fallback can read it. The job's stdout is the serving log, kept under the checkout's figures directory for the receipt. Tunnel: a --playable target on the imas-codex tunnel CLI, same-port forward with the remote end bound to the node squeue reports for nova-playable, so the browser on the laptop opens http://localhost:18506/playable and its websocket rides the same forward; this is imas-codex's file and is requested from that project's live coordinator rather than written here. Client: the browser is the client; key presses travel to the server as Bokeh UI events over the websocket and the flux map, separatrix, control points, compensating currents and keyframe wall come back as column patches, so the round trip a person feels is the tunnel's round trip plus the keyframe wall of §3. Nothing on the login node relays traffic; it only hosts the ssh endpoint and the scheduler.

Deliverables. A. The launcher and its status read: an sbatch script and a small status command that submits the job, waits for the server to accept a connection on the node, writes the allocation record, and reports node and port; measured by one real submission serving the §4 document on the H200 (a request from the login node to the node's port returns the Bokeh page, the log shows the carrier loaded once and the compilation cache reporting hits on the second keyframe) and by the job surviving the submitting shell. B. The tunnel target in imas-codex (foreign-owner). C. The live receipt: with the job live and the tunnel up from the laptop, one recorded steering session on 22086/43 in which every action logs its keyframe wall, trips and server-side event-to-push latency, the tunnel round trip is measured from the client, and the recording is the §5 session file, so the claim that a person steers the equilibrium at human response time is a receipt read from the client side rather than a projection from the node.

§7 — The page: the solve on the left, the decoded MAST camera on the right

The lead's direction of 2026-09-05 23:00: the Bokeh layout carries a second window for the decoded MAST video that imas-ambix's flux-conditioned decoder produces from the flux-surface solve. The physics and the picture sit side by side, driven by the same keyframe: every key press re-solves, the frame of §5 goes to the poloidal view and to the decoder, and the decoded camera image comes back to the second panel. The decoder is imas-ambix's deliverable (physics-carried-playable-plasma §4 and §5); this plan owns the layout, the seam it plugs into, and a placeholder that renders when no decoder is present.

Layout. One Bokeh document: a row of the poloidal figure (square, no axes, the §4 renderer) and the camera figure (the camera's own aspect, no axes, one image_rgba glyph fed from a single-cell ColumnDataSource), above a row of two readout strips: the key legend with the commanded parameter values and the compensating current per circuit as thin bars; the keyframe wall and trips per action as a sparkline with the record and playback strip (record toggle, frame counter, playback slider over the §5 session, export). A status line under the camera carries the frame index, the decode latency, the keyframe wall, and a HELD marker when the solve refuses or stagnates so the last good frame is shown and never interpolated (the imas-ambix rule). The app serves the same document with a view request argument: the default is both panels; camera renders the camera panel and its status line alone, so a second browser window on another screen shows the picture while the first carries the controls, both reading one server-side session holder, both over the one tunnelled port of §6.

The seam. Nova never imports imas-ambix (the coupled-repository rule runs one way), so the camera panel is fed through a protocol the app owns: a FrameDecoder with decode(frame: SteeringFrame) -> DecodedFrame, where the result carries an RGB image array (height, width, 3, uint8), the decode wall, and the decoder identity (checkpoint, VQ decoder, corpus digest) that the receipt of §5 records beside each frame. The app takes the decoder as a dotted path at launch (--decoder imas_ambix.…:FluxConditionedDecoder) and loads it once per session on the same H200 as the solve; with no decoder given it uses the placeholder, a grey frame carrying the frame index, so the layout, the keys and the recording work before the decoder exists. Per keyframe the loop calls the decoder after the poloidal push, off the solve's critical path, so a slow decode delays only the picture. imas-ambix implements the protocol against the §5 frame and consumes the same file a recorded session writes; the latency both sides report comes from the same clock (nova's keyframe wall, the decoder's decode wall, the page's event-to-patch time from §6c).

Deliverables. A. The two-panel layout with the camera-only view argument, the decoder protocol and the placeholder, the status line and the readout strips, measured by the session test driving ten keys through a stub solver and the placeholder decoder with every pushed column shaped as its glyph binds and the camera image updated once per keyframe, and by a rendered PNG of the page on the Solov'ev fixture committed for the record. B. The decoder implementation (foreign-owner, imas-ambix). C. The live picture: the §6c receipt extended with the decoded stream on 22086/43 and the decode wall per frame beside the keyframe wall.

§8 — The forward solve as a labeller: batch throughput and the decoder corpus

The lead's direction of 2026-09-05 23:10: imas-ambix trains the video decoder, nova supplies the labels, and the decoder works from the physics state (the flux map, the LCFS, the divertor strike points, the O-point and the X-points), never from pixels. This section asks what it costs to run the forward solve as that labeller over the decoder's corpus: how fast the solve labels a slice, whether that rate can feed training, and whether the labels must be written to file ahead of training or could be produced online. The lead authorises using the original EFIT plasma centre height as a constraint row to stabilise the solve, since the vertical mode is what the Newton walk spends its trips on, and the constrained route of §3 already carries that row.

The contract as it stands. On the training side the label a frame carries is the §5 SteeringFrame record: raster psi, psi_norm, domain_label and separatrix on the rectangular grid, the O-point, the two X-point slots, the strike points, the LCFS polyline, the coil currents and the request identity, written as a session with time as the last axis in the imas-ambix key vocabulary. On the evaluation side imas-ambix's equilibrium_labels.py reads the L2 reconstruction store into a 14-component target per camera frame time (axis R and Z, an unordered set of up to two nulls, eight LCFS radii at fixed poloidal angles about the axis, with a finite mask) and declares that store evaluator-only: a third-party label that scores the model and is never a model input. The forward labeller sits on the training side, so its output is admissible conditioning, with one caveat the authorisation carries: a solve stabilised on the EFIT centre height inherits one scalar of the reconstruction per slice, and whether that touches the evaluator firewall is imas-ambix's judgement to record, not nova's.

What is already measured, and the arithmetic it implies. The constrained reduced route solves a warm keyframe on 22086/43 in three trips of about 50 ms and converges to nanometres (§3). A labeller stepping through one shot's slices in time order warm-starts each slice from the previous one, so the expected cost per slice is one to three trips, 50 to 150 ms, once the program for that shot's operator is compiled; the operator is a trace constant today, so each distinct operator compiles once per session (minutes cold, seconds from the persistent cache) and every slice of a shot then runs the same program with its coil currents as arguments. The corpus census counts 1,341,435 slices; at 10 to 20 slices per second on one H200 that is 19 to 37 hours of one GPU for a complete pass, or an eighth of that across the reservation's eight GPUs, while a training loop that decodes at even eight frames per second per process cannot wait tens of milliseconds for a label. So the prior reading is that the labels are produced once, ahead of training, written to file per shot in the §5 session shape, and the study below measures whether the prior holds and what would change it: a batched entry over the slices of one shot (the same operator, different currents) is the lever that could take the rate from tens to hundreds of slices per second, and the reduced route has no batched entry yet.

Deliverables. A. The contract scout: what the decoder training corpus actually consumes and at which frame times (the manifest and cohort the physics-carried-playable-plasma plan names, the camera frame times per shot, the shots the frozen-six response carrier covers and how many corpus shots share an operator), what the efm store carries for the plasma centre height per slice, and the exact file the labeller must write, as a report with the field list and the shot and slice counts. B. The throughput study on the H200: one shot's slices in time order through the constrained reduced route, warm-started slice to slice, free against the EFIT centre-height row, recording per slice the trips, Newton steps, wall, converged and qualified flags and the compensating current, the compile and cache-hit cost per program, and a probe of a batched entry over eight and sixteen slices of one shot if the route admits it, with the receipt extrapolated to slices per GPU-hour and to the corpus; the report answers the three questions in numbers. C. The dataset, conditional on B: the labeller sharded across H200 jobs over the decoder's shot list, each shot's session file written as it lands with a digest manifest, resumable, in the shape A names, so imas-ambix trains on exactly what the forward solve produced.

§ Followups

Augment the reduced Newton route with the constraint rows

The constraint interface augments the Newton–Krylov ladder today. Pose the bounding-box rows and their compensating unknowns on the reduced-state plain-Newton solve in nova/equilibrium/reduced_newton.py so a constrained keyframe runs on the millisecond route, with a CPU contract on the Solov'ev machine and one H200 receipt on 22086/43 giving trips and wall per constrained re-solve. Sequence after the bounding-box rows land.

/reckon-ship playable-forward-solve §3

Landed at 81bec10b, 672f3b43, d5e90aea, merged at f3e9d85f, evidence anchor constrained-route. solve_constrained_reduced_newton extends the amplitude vector by one compensating unknown per row and the residual by the authored rows on a trip driver shared with the unconstrained entry; reachable as route reduced_newton through ForwardProfile.solve. CPU 6 of 6 on the Solov'ev machine: empty tuple bit-identical on every non-timing leaf, row at the free centroid one trip and zero steps, row response 2.6e-7 against a central difference at the converged extremum, moved target reached to 1e-6 m, warm start no more trips and strictly fewer Newton steps (the seed shadow is already converged, so every solve settles in one trip). H200 job 1263043 on 22086/43: centroid moved by 1 and 2 cm, three trips and 25 steps each, achieved within 2.3e-9 m, compensating current -240.8 and -305.2 A on the derived up-down antisymmetric direction, median warm trip 49.9 and 50.9 ms against 47.2 ms free. The bounding-box rows join as further pairs when their node merges; the row landed here is the position row.

Constraint targets as traced kernel arguments so a moved target does not recompile

Found by the coordinator's read of the constrained route: the row target and scale enter the compiled kernels through the pair closed over in _RowAugmentation.rows (jnp.asarray(pair.binding.target)), and the initial unknown likewise, so every moved target is a new trace constant and every keyframe pays a first-trip compile. The H200 receipt shows it: the second move's first trip cost 16.6 s although its warm trips cost 51 ms. Make the target, the scale and the initial unknown per row traced arguments of every kernel in nova/equilibrium/reduced_newton.py (threaded like the frozen shadow and base state already are), so one compiled program serves every keyframe of a session. Contract: on the Solov'ev machine two consecutive moved targets produce exactly one compilation of each kernel (count through jax compile hooks or a cache-hit count), decisions and terminal flux bit-identical to the current code on the same inputs; on the H200 the driver benchmarks/reduced_newton_constrained_rows.py re-run on 22086/43 shows the second and third moves' first-trip wall at the warm-trip figure (tens of milliseconds, not seconds), the receipt persisted beside the current one. Sequence before the keyframe receipt (§4b) and the live receipt (§6c), which both need it.

The poloidal view in the locked style: clipped hex cells, unfilled contours, wall, coils, null markers

Apply decision plasma-view-style to the shared poloidal renderer the §4a loop introduces (apps/pulsedesign/poloidal_view.py, consumed by apps/playable). Reuse, not reproduction: the pulse-design app already turns the plasma frame's polygons into a Bokeh multi_polygons with fill_alpha per cell (apps/pulsedesign/simulator.py plasma_data through polygeo.polygons) and hides the axis with axis.visible = False; imas-ink's InkStyle (imas_ink/style.py) is the reference for weights and markers (coil edge grey with no face, wall a thin dark line, axis marker a dot, X-point marker an x, figure drawn with ax.axis("off")). The view: no axes, grid, frame or bounding box; the first-wall profile as one dark grey polyline; each coil as a simple outline with no fill; the plasma as light purple hex cells at one alpha, where the cells the separatrix cuts are drawn as their clipped polygons from the solve's clipped supports (nova/equilibrium/separatrix_clip.py, ClippedSupports and TracedClippedSupports vertices, the same cut the solve integrates over) so the plasma edge is the solved boundary, not a stair; psi contour lines in dark grey, unfilled, at a fixed count of levels between axis and boundary with the separatrix as the outermost; markers on the primary X-point and the O-point read from the frame's labelled points, with the secondary X-point and strike points shown lighter when present. Measures in tests/test_playable_session.py or a sibling test: on the Solov'ev fixture's solved frame the pushed columns give one clipped polygon per cut cell whose vertices lie on the separatrix within the lattice pitch, no cell polygon crosses the separatrix by more than that pitch, the contour source carries the stated level count with the separatrix level present, the coil and wall sources carry outlines only (no fill columns), the figure has no visible axis, and a rendered PNG of that frame is committed under docs/figures/playable-forward-solve/view/ for the record. Sequence after §4a lands, on the same files.

The labeller at 300 slices a second: slices batched on device with the trip loop inside the program

Lead target 2026-09-06: about 300 slices a second, against 12 to 14 warm on one H200 today (48 to 50 ms a trip, one to two trips a slice). A dense solve on about 1,100 plasma-cell amplitudes is well under a millisecond on the device, so the trip is latency-bound: sequential passes (the Newton ladder as a host loop with data-dependent backtracking, the trips of the topology read) each pay kernel-launch latency of about 7.6 microseconds a step and the host round trips between them. The vmap probe over the fused trip ran slower per element because the fused close is a write-then-read topology pass whose per-element memory dominates, so batching needs the pass re-cast before it pays. Three steps, each its own node with a receipt on 22086: (1) after f-pfs-016, the whole slice solve inside one compiled program, a fixed trip budget with masked convergence instead of the host ladder, so one call is one slice and the launch count per slice drops to a handful; (2) the topology read of a batch of slices as a parallel pass (fixed-iteration label propagation over the lattice, no per-slice sequential flood), bit-identical classification to the current read on the bank rows; (3) vmap of the whole slice program over 64 to 256 slices of the corpus with the currents, initial state and targets as batch arguments, measured as slices a second per GPU at batch 1, 16, 64, 256. The fence is 300 slices a second on one H200 at the largest batch, or the measured curve with the step that saturates named. The free arm only, EFIT-free.

Shape control through the pulse-design inverse step: coil currents from the bounding-box targets, forward solve on the prescribed currents

Decision shape-control-formulation. Build the control step the original pulse-design app runs (InverseDesign.solve_current in nova/equilibrium/inverse.py: Tikhonov-regularised least squares through MoorePenrose with gamma scaled by the plasma current and field rows weighted by sqrt(field_weight)) on the forward operator: the response of the boundary flux at the four bounding-box points, the radial field at the outer and inner points and the vertical field at the upper and lower points, plus the X-point null, to every free circuit current, read off the operator's response carrier and the lattice interpolation the constraint module already uses (sample_lattice_flux, the field reads of FieldComponentConstraint), with the plasma's own contribution at the current iterate subtracted. One module by what it does (for example nova/equilibrium/shape_inverse.py) returning the prescribed current vector for a commanded BoundingBoxTarget. The app's ProductionSolver then runs: inverse step from the commanded shape, forward solve on the reduced route with those prescribed currents warm-started from the previous frame, one more inverse step at the achieved plasma if the turning-point error exceeds a stated tolerance; no constraint pairs on the control path. Bulk R and Z become rigid moves of the four points. Contracts on the Solov'ev CPU fixture: the inverse step reproduces the seed's own currents from its own turning points, a commanded 2 cm raise of the upper point is achieved within 2 mm after at most two inverse-forward rounds, the fast subset unchanged. Receipt on the H200 on 22086/43 through the app: the same two arms as the shape-row receipt (upper point plus 2 cm, elongation plus 5 percent) with previous, commanded, achieved turning points, the coil currents per circuit, trips and wall per round, persisted under docs/figures/playable-forward-solve/shape-inverse/. Landed as infrastructure with a negative shape-motion gate (main b8f06271, worktree tip cfd79b37, node pfs-inverse-step-shape-control-sol-2, codex sol over two lanes, 31 commits). nova/equilibrium/shape_inverse.py implements the pulse-design inverse anchored to the original seed: rows are the isoflux and Br/Bz conditions at the bounding-box points with the plasma and fixed circuits on the right-hand side, sqrt(50) on field rows, Tikhonov gamma x |Ip| on the CHANGE from the seed (the reference InverseDesign penalises total current because it designs from nothing; penalising total current here picked a different minimum-norm coil set and drifted the boundary 0.1 Wb), three placement rounds without a nonlinear solve, one forward solve after, wired into apps/playable. The app pushes a SteeringFrame per keyframe and the renderers draw from it

Decision prototype-data-through-contract. PlayableSession assembles a SteeringFrame after every solve (through assemble_frame, given a receipt path from the reduced-route result the app holds, or a frame builder from the equilibrium where the receipt type is not available), records it in the session store when recording is on, and frame_push is replaced by a reduction of the frame to the renderer channels: the plasma cells still come from the operator's clipped geometry, but the contours are the frame's surfaces, the separatrix, LCFS, axis, X-points and strike points are the frame's fields, and the camera decoder receives the same frame. Contracts: the session test drives ten keys and every pushed column is shaped as its glyph binds; a recorded session read back through frames_from_session reproduces the pushed channels; the keyframe receipt on the H200 (f-pfs-017's driver) re-run shows the frame assembly cost per keyframe. Sequence after f-pfs-021 (the surface block) and after the two-panel node releases apps/playable/session.py and apps/playable/main.py.

The psi unit label in the session schema: Wb or Wb per radian, one answer under COCOS 17

nova/equilibrium/steering_frames.py declares COCOS 17 in its header and says psi is Wb, while its variable table labels the raster psi Wb (per radian). COCOS 17 has the poloidal flux as the total flux in Wb (the 2 pi convention e_Bp = 1), and the raster psi assembled from the solve is the total flux in Wb as the forward operator carries it. Settle the label from the values: assert on the Solov'ev fixture that the frame's psi at the axis equals the solve's axis flux in Wb, fix the label everywhere in the schema and the netCDF attributes, and state the convention once in the module header so imas-ambix reads one answer. Small; belongs with the next touch of the schema.

A shape-steering row set for the inverse step: more points, field rows that separate the vertical mode, X-point handling

The seed-anchored inverse (main b8f06271) reproduces the seed and stays within 20 kA per circuit but turns a 20 mm top raise into a near-rigid vertical shift and loses the axis on an elongation command. The receipts (docs/figures/playable-forward-solve/shape-inverse/) show why: with four turning points and two X-point null rows the drivable spectrum spans 3.6 decades and a rigid shift closes the exercised rows as well as a shape change does. The batched labeller engine: many shots per compiled step, sharded across the job's devices

New module nova/equilibrium/batched_labeller.py on top of the compiled slice route (solve_reduced_newton_compiled and solve_constrained_reduced_newton_compiled in nova/equilibrium/reduced_newton.py, lines 2241 and 2292; _compiled_slice_solver at 1193 builds the fixed-shape program). Element = one slice of one shot with traced arguments prescribed currents (101,), target current, requested class, initial state and, for conditioning, the centroid target. (1) Batch axis over B elements by vmap of the compiled program (or a batched program built once with a leading axis), the operator replicated; the topology read stays per element inside the program (the bounded parallel propagation is not exact, see f-pfs-025). (2) Device-side branch guard: current centroid of the terminal state against the reference (B,) with the 50 mm tolerance, then a masked second pass with the centroid row imposed for the failing or non-converged elements only. (3) Sharding: jax.sharding.Mesh over every visible device with axis batch, NamedSharding(PartitionSpec(batch)) on element arrays, replicated operator; jax.jit with in_shardings and out_shardings (jax 0.11; jax.shard_map is available if needed). (4) Returns per element the terminal state, converged, termination reason, trips, terminal residual, achieved centroid, conditioned flag and the labelled topology fields the frame needs, in one device-to-host read per step. CPU contracts on the Solovev fixture with XLA_FLAGS=--xla_force_host_platform_device_count=2: a batch of two identical elements sharded over two devices returns the sequential compiled route's terminal state bit for bit; a padded (inactive) element changes nothing; the masked conditioning pass matches the sequential constrained route. H200 measurement in ONE job with --gres=gpu:3 (--cpus-per-task=12 --mem=256G, JAX_PLATFORMS=cuda,cpu inside the wrapped command, explicit checkout root): slices a second at 1, 2 and 3 devices for B per device of 16, 64 and 256 over 48 corpus shots, compile wall separately, nvidia-smi utilisation sampled per arm, converged and guard fractions identical to the sequential route; report the best configuration. Backend codex luna at high effort. Write paths: nova/equilibrium/batched_labeller.py, tests/test_batched_labeller.py, benchmarks/batched_labeller_receipt.py, docs/figures/playable-forward-solve/batched-labeller/, the report; reduced_newton.py only by scope request. The forward solve admits a magnetic axis under reversed plasma current

On the reversed-current MAST block 22475 to 22626 (efm Ip -1.0 to -0.1 MA) every free solve raises NoQualifiedAxisError at the seed although the seed flux is finite and boundary-bounded, because the O-point census and the axis-admission rule in nova/equilibrium/topology.py (with the labeller admission in scripts/labeller_batch/shard.py) assume the positive-current flux sign: with Ip reversed the axis is a flux minimum and the boundary value sits below the map. Make the axis and saddle read sign-aware (select the extremum by the sign of the plasma current, or by the sign of the seed flux at the stored efm axis, and flip the qualification inequalities accordingly), keep every positive-current receipt bit-identical (the twelve bank rows, the Solovev certificate and the hex-reference tests are the referees), add a Solovev fixture with reversed current asserting the same axis and residual to sign, then re-solve three block shots (22475, 22550, 22626) through shard.py on one H200 card and report their converged fractions against the zero baseline. Spec-level guided; codex luna; the labeller relabels the block once this lands.
/reckon-ship playable-forward-solve §8

Landed as 749816ae and merged, and the physics answer is negative. The axis and saddle read is now sign-aware in the way that survives review: it selects the extremum whose curvature index matches the plasma-current polarity, which is gauge-free, rather than inferring polarity from the sign of the axis flux, which the first implementation did and which the coordinator refused because nova's poloidal flux carries the Biot-Savart gauge. Scope was extended mid-flight to scripts/labeller_batch/shard.py and the parity benchmark after the node's own blocker found no caller ever supplied a reversed sign, so the corrected read was unreachable and its first relabel had measured code no shot could enter. So the plumbing hypothesis is eliminated and the block's failure is a solver or physics question, opened as its own followup. The block stays excluded from cohorts.

The labeller sessions carry an empty LCFS: fill the polyline from the outermost traced surface when the raster is off

Found by the media session on 2026-09-07 and confirmed by the coordinator on corpus file 18746.nc: every rasterless labeller session has boundary_vertex dimension 0, lcfs_r shape (0, n_t) and n_boundary_coords 0 on every frame, although the contract (steering_frames.py header, decision contract-surface-layout) promises the LCFS polyline and the flux_surface arrays do carry the psi_norm 1.0 surface (64 rays). The writer derives lcfs from the raster separatrix, which is absent when include_raster is false, so the polyline is silently empty and a consumer must rebuild it from the outermost nested surface. Fix in nova/equilibrium/steering_frames.py assemble_frame (and the writer path shard.py uses): when no raster separatrix exists, set lcfs_r and lcfs_z to the traced psi_norm 1.0 surface (closed, 64 vertices plus the repeat point if the schema expects closure) with n_boundary_coords accordingly; keep the raster-derived polyline when the raster is present so rastered sessions are unchanged; add a test writing a rasterless Solovev session and asserting a non-empty, closed LCFS that matches the outermost flux surface to 1e-12; do not touch the running corpus writer (the change applies at the next relaunch, and the receipt may later backfill). Consumers tonight already read the outermost surface, so this is a contract repair, not a training blocker. Spec-level exact; local lane.

The regularised inverse step drags uncommanded turning points and loses the axis: bound the achieved shape, not the currents

Measured by the regularisation node (receipts under docs/figures/playable-forward-solve/shape-inverse/): at the weight that keeps every circuit change inside 20 kA, an upper-point plus 20 mm command achieves 31.1 mm on the commanded coordinate and moves an uncommanded coordinate 124 mm, and an elongation plus 5 percent command produces prescribed currents whose forward solve raises NoQualifiedAxisError, so the command has no achieved shape at all. Both are properties of the inverse step, not of the penalty: the least squares is solved against the commanded rows with no term holding the unexercised rows in place, and nothing bounds the step to the neighbourhood where the forward map still admits an axis. Two parts, each with a receipt on the same three commands. (1) Penalise the achieved boundary rather than the currents: include every turning-point row in the residual with the uncommanded rows targeted at their previous values, so the step trades commanded motion against unwanted motion explicitly, and report per-command commanded, achieved and uncommanded drift as a table. (2) Bound the step by admissibility: after the inverse step, verify the forward solve admits an axis and contract the step (line search on the current delta) until it does, recording the accepted fraction; the elongation command is the referee because it fails outright today. Keep the null invariant exact and the 20 kA ceiling. Gate: tests/test_shape_inverse.py green on all_debug plus the three receipts with the drift table; an H200 arm only once every command has an achieved shape. Spec-level guided; codex terra, same member.

The second-null slot has no wall containment, so the labeller writes X-points inside the centre column and outboard of the wall

Confirmed in the source after a measurement from the media session: Topology.x_point_index in nova/equilibrium/topology.py excludes a candidate outside the wall polygon before the flux ranking runs, and its docstring records why, but ForwardSolve._secondary_x_point in nova/equilibrium/forward.py qualifies candidates on two conditions only, finite coordinates and distance from the primary. There is no containment test, so the second slot takes whatever saddle scores highest anywhere in the domain. Measured against each shot's own 36-point efm limiter (wall R 0.195 to 1.900): 25 of 208 finite X-points outside on 21858, 15 of 204 on 27079, 16 of 92 on 22086, 2 of 148 on 24854. They fail in both directions, which is what rules out a wall-resolution explanation: three shots put them just inboard at R 0.165 to 0.181 against the 0.195 inboard wall, inside the centre column where no null can exist, while 22086 puts them outboard at R 1.912 to 1.937 against the 1.900 outboard wall and within 7 mm of the midplane, which reads as a search that ran to the domain edge and returned the boundary rather than failing. EFIT's own two X-point slots on the same shots fall outside on zero of 257 rows, so the reference reconstruction never does this and the solve does. This is the recorded defect class, the typed-saddle selector without containment that picked out-of-vessel saddles on 8 of 12 MAST arms; the polygon-containment remedy landed in the primary selector and not in this path. Fix: qualify the secondary candidates with _points_inside_polygon from nova/equilibrium/connectivity_boundary.py against self.operator.topology.wall.coordinate, alongside the finite and distinct conditions, returning NaN when nothing qualifies rather than the best out-of-vessel candidate. Test on a fixture whose highest-scoring distinct saddle sits outside the limiter, asserting the slot is NaN rather than that point, plus a diverted fixture asserting the in-vessel second null is unchanged. Keep every positive receipt identical. The corpus already written carries these rows in slot 1; anything deriving a leg, a strike geometry or a connection length from slot 1 inherits them silently, so the consumer is told and the next writer relaunch carries the fix. Strike points are deliberately out of scope: a strike point lies on the boundary and the stored limiter is a coarse outline, so containment is the wrong test for it. Spec-level exact; local lane.

The session carries no wall, so a consumer cannot test containment against the polygon the solve used

A rasterless labeller session carries the axis, both X-point slots, the strike points and the flux surfaces, but no wall group. A consumer wanting to check that a labelled point lies inside the vessel therefore has to fetch the machine's level-2 limiter itself, which for MAST is a 36-point outline, while the polygon nova's own primary saddle selector tests against is the forward operator's wall.coordinate, the wall node set of the trimmed hexagonal tiling. Those are not the same polygon and the finer one is the authoritative one, so every consumer-side containment filter is currently approximate by construction and no consumer can reproduce the solve's own admission decision. Add the wall polygon to the session as its own group, closed, with the vertex count, the source identity it came from and the same COCOS and unit declarations the rest of the schema carries, written once per session rather than per frame since it does not move. Then state in the schema header that this polygon, not a machine-description limiter, is the containment reference for the labelled points, and give the receipt a per-shot count of labelled points outside it so the writer reports its own containment rather than leaving each consumer to discover it. This is what makes the secondary-null containment repair checkable from the data instead of only from the source. Test on a rasterless Solovev session asserting the group round-trips closed with the operator's own vertices to 1e-12, plus a count assertion on a fixture carrying one deliberately out-of-vessel point. Spec-level exact; local lane; the change applies at the next writer relaunch.

Is the 4 to 6 cm axis disagreement with EFIT a definition difference or a solve gap

The consumer's fidelity gate scores nova against the EFIT referee on 51 free converged flat-top slices and the axis misses a 2 cm bound on 48 of them, median 4.56 cm, p90 5.75, max 6.24, while the boundary radius RMS is inside the bound on 24 and reaches per-shot medians of 1.73 cm on 21989 and 1.90 cm on 22086. Definitions, referee and flat-top window are recorded under the consumer-fidelity-gate-against-the-efit-referee anchor of the landed record. The shape of that result argues against a fidelity gap: a solve with the plasma in the wrong place moves the boundary with the axis, and here the boundary is often inside the bound while the axis is uniformly outside it, which is the signature of two different definitions of the same word. Settle it rather than carrying it as a caveat. Read the signed R and Z components of the axis difference over the same slices: a definition difference shows as a consistent offset in one component across shots and a fidelity gap does not. Score the same slices against the alternative referents the session already carries, current_centroid and reference_centroid_z, and against the referee's own axis definition read from its documentation rather than inferred, naming exactly which quantity EFIT publishes as the magnetic axis and which quantity nova returns. State the outcome as one of three: a definition difference, in which case the gate's axis metric is comparing the wrong pair and the fix is the comparison rather than the solver; a real offset, in which case it is a solver node with the boundary agreement to explain; or a mixture with the split quantified. The consumer is re-scoring on a denominator that rises to about 70 as wrongly excluded rows are admitted, which changes the fractions and not the direction, so this node does not wait on that. Spec-level guided; the measurement is cheap and CPU-bound on sessions already written.

The axis admission refuses EFIT's own reconstruction flux at flat top: qualify the candidate census against a map that has an axis

Measured and recorded under the axis-admission-refuses-the-efit-map-at-flat-top anchor of the landed record. On the six frozen carriers, all ordinary positive-current shots, the free solve fails more often than it converges inside the flat-top window on four of six, and where it fails the conditioning cannot derive its centroid target because the axis census returns no qualified candidate with a resolved component. The writer computes that conditioning seed before the free solve runs, as the carried warm start when one exists and otherwise the slice's EFIT reconstruction flux, and the warm start is cleared by any unclean slice, so after the first failure in a run the refused map is EFIT's own — a map whose axis EFIT published and the fidelity referee reads. The refusal is therefore a property of the admission rule and not of a broken intermediate state.

Do this as a diagnosis before any repair, because the rule has several gates and only one of them is failing. Take one refused slice per shot, seeded from the EFIT reconstruction flux, and print the full candidate census the qualification consumes: every O-point candidate with its position, flux, curvature index, resolution state and whichever per-candidate validity flags the census carries, alongside EFIT's published axis for the same slice. Then state which gate rejects the candidate that sits at EFIT's axis, naming it: the resolution or nonsingularity test, the extremum-type test, the in-material or wall containment test, the flux ordering against the boundary, or the absence of any candidate near that position at all. That last case is a different defect from the others and must be distinguished, because it means the stationary-point finder missed the point rather than the qualifier rejecting it. Only then propose the repair, and gate it on the twelve positive-current bank rows and the Solovev certificate staying bit-identical, plus the refused slices converging or their refusal being re-attributed to a named physical cause. Report the per-shot converged fraction over the six carriers before and after.

Two constraints. Sequencing: the reversed-current polarity node holds nova/equilibrium/topology.py and nova/equilibrium/stencil_nulls.py, so this node starts only once that has landed and merged. And this is a diagnosis node whose deliverable is the named gate with its census evidence, not a fix; the repair is its successor, because a repair proposed without knowing which gate fires would be a guess against a rule that has at least five.

The achieved-centroid read runs an unconstrained topology read, so a converged slice aborts on its own flux

Established in the source, not inferred. The writer reads the achieved current centroid through ForwardProfile.current_moment_observation, which has no requested_class parameter at all and so forwards None into _integral_state, whose own default is None. A topology read with no requested class has to find its boundary emergently, which is strictly harder than being told the class, while the free solve that produced the flux ran with an explicit per-slice requested class. The same converged flux is therefore read twice under two different admission regimes and the second refuses, raising a no-qualified-axis error inside the free-solve try and aborting a slice whose solve had converged. On the six frozen carriers this costs 119 of 372 free-converged rows, roughly a third, with per-shot counts recorded in the landed record. It is an API asymmetry between a solve and its own diagnostic read; nothing about it involves EFIT or the reversed-current block.

Two halves, and the first is the one that must not be skipped. Give current_moment_observation a requested_class parameter that it forwards to _integral_state, and audit every sibling on that surface for the same omission, naming each one checked, because a diagnostic read that silently disagrees with the solve it describes is a defect class rather than one call site: current_moment_map is the immediate neighbour and there may be others. Then have both writers pass the class they solved with, so the achieved read reproduces the solve's admission decision. Gate on the twelve positive-current bank rows and the Solovev certificate staying bit-identical, on the aborted rows of one carrier now yielding a finite achieved centroid, and on the per-shot converged fraction over the six carriers before and after; report whether any row that previously produced a centroid changes it at all, since a change there would mean the class argument alters the moment rather than only the admission. State plainly if some aborted rows still refuse with the class supplied, because that residue is then a genuine solver question and worth its own node with a much smaller denominator.

Sequencing: this touches nova/equilibrium/forward.py, which the secondary-null containment node currently holds, and the writers, so it starts once that has landed. It does not need the topology files and so does not queue behind the polarity node. Worth doing early regardless of the throughput work, because recovering a third of the slices of a shot is a larger corpus gain than any rate change now on the table. Spec-level exact.

Reversed plasma current fails the branch guard before convergence, with polarity plumbing eliminated

The app's own steering has never been receipted, which is the half of demo readiness nobody measured

A gap in the demo-readiness answer given to the lead, found by re-reading what the existing receipts actually cover. The keyframe cost is measured and being re-measured. The steering is not, in the app's own configuration, and a fast keyframe that steers wrongly still fails a demo, so this is the more important half.

What exists and why none of it answers the question. The centroid-row route is receipted and excellent: on 22086/43 a plus one centimetre and a plus two centimetre command each reached the target to within nanometres, every move converged and every row qualified. That is not the app's route; decision shape-control-formulation reserved compensating rows for diagnostic placement. The app steers through solve_shape_inverse followed by a forward solve on the prescribed currents, with a step-fraction cap and no delta regularisation, since its penalty defaults to zero and the app does not pass it. The measurements that exist for the shape-inverse route are under other configurations and are poor: at the regularisation weight that keeps circuit changes inside 20 kA, a 20 mm upper-point command achieved 31 mm while dragging an uncommanded turning point 124 mm, and an elongation command produced currents whose forward solve admitted no axis; the unregularised row set requested 1.9 to 7.9 MA on the same commands. An earlier shape-inverse receipt was headlined as steering in the commanded direction when the boundary had moved about a fifth of the command. So every figure for this route comes from a configuration the app does not use, in both directions.

Measure the app's configuration exactly as it runs. On the frozen-six carrier on one card, drive three commands through apps/playable/production.py as the app constructs it, with its own gamma, its own step fraction and its own reference current, and report per command the previous, commanded and achieved turning points, the drift on every uncommanded coordinate, the maximum circuit change against the 20 kA ceiling, and whether the forward solve admitted an axis. State the achieved fraction of each command as a number, and state the largest uncommanded drift as a number, because those two together are what an audience sees. Do not tune anything; the deliverable is what the app does today. Reuse the member that holds the keyframe node so the machine is built once and the compilation cache is warm, and run it after that node lands rather than beside it. Spec-level exact.

The camera decode blocks the key callback, so a slow decode delays the physics picture it was ordered behind

A defect in this app found by the consumer naming its reading of our loop as load-bearing rather than assuming it, and then checking the source rather than the docstring. Both the module docstring of apps/playable/main.py and the comment inside the key callback state that the decode runs after the poloidal push so a slow decode delays only the picture. The ordering is real and the conclusion does not follow. on_key is one synchronous Bokeh callback: the solve runs, the poloidal channels are assigned to their sources, and then session.decode_frame() is called. There is no next-tick callback, no timeout callback and no document-lock release anywhere in the app package. Bokeh holds the document lock for the duration of a synchronous callback and flushes the accumulated change set when it returns, so the poloidal assignment does not reach the browser until the decode has finished, and neither does the status line, and the next keypress is not processed meanwhile. Ordering statements inside a callback describe the server's sequence, not what a viewer sees.

The measured stakes, from the consumer: its transformer token decode is a median 42 to 50 ms per frame, which fits inside a keypress, and its VQ pixel decode is about 1.8 seconds, which does not. Under the current loop that 1.8 seconds is a playability blocker rather than a smoothness cost, and it blocks the physics picture the ordering was written to protect.

Fix on this side without changing the decoder protocol, which the consumer's decoder already implements. Move the decode out of the key callback: either defer it with a next-tick callback so the poloidal flush reaches the browser first and the camera source is patched on the following tick, or run it off the document lock and patch the camera source when it completes. Keep the held behaviour, where a refused solve pushes nothing and the status says HELD, and keep the decode wall and decoder identity in the receipt. Correct both the docstring and the comment in the same commit, because they are what made a false claim look verified. Gate: a test driving two keys in succession with a deliberately slow stub decoder asserting the poloidal sources are updated before the second key is accepted, plus the existing app tests unchanged. Spec-level exact.

/reckon-ship playable-forward-solve §3

Deferred by the lead's reorder of 2026-09-07: the playable demo urgency has passed and the work returns to a robust forward model for labelling. The finding stands and is correct: the key callback is synchronous with no deferral anywhere in the package, so Bokeh flushes nothing until it returns and a slow decode delays the physics picture rather than only the camera. Reopen with the re-seed flag it was paired with if a steering demo is ever revived.

Validate the current-centroid major radius, which is inboard of EFIT on every slice measured

Nova's current-centroid major radius is inboard of EFIT's published current_centrd_r on all 51 evidence slices of the six carriers, median 5.62 cm, mean 6.06, standard deviation 3.14, per-shot medians from 2.68 cm on 21978 to 8.67 on 21986, zero of 51 outboard. A fraction of exactly zero is a systematic sign, not scatter. The height component has always been guarded, by the branch guard and the conditioning target, and the major radius has never been compared with anything, which is why a several-centimetre offset survived a corpus of 923 shots.

Two things to do and they are separable. First, validate: assert the centroid major radius against the EFIT value on the twelve bank rows and on a sample across the six carriers, with a tolerance argued from the grid spacing rather than fitted to the current miss, and record the measured distribution beside it. A guard that is set to pass today's number would ratify the defect. Second, attribute: the centroid comes from the same moment observation that runs an unconstrained topology read, so if its domain mask is the emergent boundary where the solve used the requested class, the moment integral is over the wrong support and the centroid is displaced systematically, which is the observed shape. Test it by supplying the class on real slices where the emergent and requested classes differ and reporting whether the major radius moves toward EFIT's. That is a different measurement from the one the class repair already gates, which asserts a bit-identical centroid when the classes agree, and both are wanted. If the class does not carry it, the next candidates are the support declaration, since the observation is taken on the all-domain support, and the cell-current weighting itself.

Sequencing: this depends on the class repair landing, since its parameter is the instrument for the attribution half. The validation half could go first and is the more urgent of the two, because a labelled channel that nothing checks is what allowed this. Spec-level guided.

/reckon-ship playable-forward-solve §8

Publish the decoder protocol as an executable conformance check, because the only call the app makes is the one nobody tested

The consumer's decoder was found to raise on the next decode after a zero-argument reset(), which is the only form this app ever calls, at session and base-observation boundaries. So the first boundary edit in front of an audience would have crashed on the far side of the seam. Its reset takes optional seed frames, the seeded call was tested, the zero-argument call was not. A second, smaller violation from the same check: it returned a module-local frame lookalike rather than apps.playable.camera.DecodedFrame, which being a named tuple would probably have duck-typed through, and so would have worked until something checked.

The consumer found both and is repairing both, and the defects are its own. The gap this exposes is ours. Nova defines the protocol, nova is the only caller, and nova has no test that any decoder satisfies the calls the app actually makes: the sole zero-argument reset in the test tree belongs to an unrelated module. We validated our own placeholder and never published the contract as something a foreign implementation could be run against, which is the same pattern as the other three defects tonight with ourselves as the unvalidated member.

Build the check as an importable conformance suite parameterised over a decoder dotted path, defaulting to the placeholder so it runs in the ordinary lane, and drive it exactly as main.py does rather than as a protocol reading suggests: construct from the dotted path with the environment configuration the app passes, decode a real SteeringFrame reconstructed from a labeller session rather than a stub, assert the returned object is our own DecodedFrame type and not merely shaped like it, assert the image is uint8 with three identical channels and the declared shape, assert the decode wall and the identity are present and the identity is stable across calls, then call reset() with no arguments and decode again and require that to succeed, then call it a second time mid-sequence as a boundary edit would and require the same. Document in the module docstring that a decoder implementer runs this against their dotted path, and say so wherever the decoder argument is documented. Gate: the suite green against the placeholder on the ordinary lane. Spec-level exact.

The pinned limited boundary takes an unfiltered wall maximum, so the centre column can bind the plasma

Full measurement and mechanism under the-pinned-limited-boundary-has-no-reachability-filter in the landed record. On 27079's guard-ok unconditioned frames the limited class has an axis-to-boundary flux span of 0.399 times EFIT's against 1.086 for diverted, and its boundary sits a median 142.9 mm and a maximum 391.6 mm from a limiter EFIT touches at 1.1 mm; the worst frame spans 1.1 mWb from axis to boundary with a boundary area 24 times too small, and the branch guard passes on every one. Mechanism candidate, from reading the source and not yet measured: with a class requested the read takes the pinned wall anchor for limited, which is the extremum of the polarity-signed flux over the wall nodes, and the wall polygon includes the centre column at R 0.195. Early in a shot, with the solenoid strongest and the plasma weakest, psi on the inboard wall can exceed the outboard contact and approach the axis, and neither the selector nor the pinned path tests that the winning node can bind the plasma. The emergent path does carry such a rule, a private-flux shadow test excluding contacts beyond the x-point band, and the pinned path the labeller uses has no equivalent.

Two steps and the first is one job. Confirm or refute: on the worst frame print the chosen wall node's R, Z and psi beside the axis psi and beside the outboard contact EFIT implies. A node near R 0.195 confirms it; an outboard node refutes it and returns the reporter's saddle-search hypothesis. Then repair by giving the pinned wall anchor a reachability test of the same kind the emergent path has, so a candidate must be able to bind the plasma rather than merely carry the extreme flux; argue the test from the geometry, not from excluding the centre column by name, since naming one structure would leave every other unreachable wall segment admitted. Gate on the twelve positive-current bank rows and the Solovev certificate staying bit-identical, on the diverted class being untouched, and on the limited frames of 27079 recovering a flux-span ratio near unity with a boundary within a stated distance of the limiter. Add a guard assertion that a limited boundary lies within a geometric tolerance of the wall, because nothing in the branch guard compares the boundary to the limiter today, which is why 28 wrong frames reported success.

Two consequences to carry into the repair record. The compressed span compresses psi_norm, so the 26 TORAX faces and their p_prime and ff_prime sit on a wrong abscissa in those frames, which corrupts the profile coordinate rather than the outline, and anything trained or scored on limited-phase labels inherits it. And the mechanism predicts the defect is worst where the consumer measured its early-phase dR structure before 0.21 s, so a confirmation collapses two findings into one cause. The media session is holding both Thomson figure regenerations until this lands and has asked to validate the limited frames against the wall as part of it. Spec-level guided.

Re-seed the decoder on every edit behind a flag, paired with taking the decode off the key callback

Pairs with the blocking-callback repair because both change the same loop and neither should land alone. The session resets the decoder's history only when the base shot or base slice changes, or when a decoder is first assigned, so a steering edit re-seeds nothing and history rolls autoregressively across every keypress. The consumer measures its decoder degrading within about four frames of leaving real-frame history and not recovering, per-frame error rising from 8.4 at the first transition to 21 by the fifth and then sitting between 13 and 46, so a steering session past about four keyframes sits in that degraded rollout with the person watching it degrade while causing it.

The consumer asked for re-seeding and gave a mechanical reason rather than a hopeful one, which is what makes it worth doing: the model takes four real history frames as input, so seeding from real frames puts it in distribution while free-running feeds it its own output and is out of distribution, which is the classic exposure-bias failure its curve describes. Re-seeding keeps every decode in the regime the model was trained for and explains why the short-horizon case measured best rather than merely landing in it. Its condition is that the sampling generator is restored deterministically on reset, which its repaired reset already does, without which consecutive frames are independent draws and the panel flickers even when the plasma barely moves.

Build it as a session option, defaulting to the current behaviour, with a free-running mode retained so the rollout can still be measured as it improves; the consumer asked for a flag rather than a replacement for that reason. Record in the schema and the report the trade it makes, which is real: re-seeding every edit converts the decoder from a dynamics model into a conditional single-frame renderer, so the frame depends on the base observation and the current boundary rather than on the sequence of edits that led there, and transient dynamics between edits are gone. For the claim this demo makes, that a person changes the boundary and sees the plasma respond, that is the honest trade, but it must be labelled as that rather than presented as temporal coherence the rollout does not have. Gate: a test asserting the reset hook fires once per edit under the flag and not at all without it, and that two identical conditioning inputs under the flag produce identical frames, which is the determinism the condition buys. Whether a frame per edit is affordable at a 1.8 second pixel decode is the callback node's question, not this one's. Spec-level exact.

The diverted class, used as the healthy control all night, drifts monotonically against EFIT

Measured in the 54,415-frame census and recorded under the-limited-defect-and-the-control-drift-are-corpus-wide. Banded in time, the diverted median span ratio against EFIT rises monotonically from 0.70 in the earliest band through 0.83 to 1.15 late: about thirty per cent low early and fifteen per cent high late, over tens of thousands of frames, crossing unity around 300 to 400 ms where it sits at 1.016. This is the class both sessions treated as the healthy control, so every ratio quoted against a diverted baseline tonight inherits it, including the figures that made cold starts look harmless, which survive only because the corpus agrees at 0.838 warm against 0.865 cold. The earliest band's control has n of 36 and must not be leaned on alone.

Diagnose before proposing anything, and do not assume it belongs to the same family as the limited defect. A trend that crosses unity is not a scale error and not a missing filter; the shape suggests a quantity that varies through a discharge rather than a defect in a branch, and the limited-branch mechanism has no route to it since the diverted boundary is the saddle anchor rather than the wall. The first task is to break the confound between time and everything that varies with time. Regress the ratio against candidate quantities measured per slice rather than against time itself: the plasma current, the solenoid current, the X-point distance from the magnetic axis, the X-point distance from the grid's refined region, the boundary elongation, and the fitted profile peaking. Report which of them, if any, explains the drift better than time does, and say plainly if none do, because a null there is a real result that rules out the cheap explanations.

Two candidates worth naming so they are tested rather than rediscovered: an X-point resolution that degrades as the saddle moves away from wherever the grid is finest, which would predict the drift tracks X-point position rather than time; and the profile interpolation, since p_prime and ff_prime arrive on the reference psi_norm grid and are read onto nova's, so a systematically changing profile shape could bias the span in a way that reverses sign as the shape evolves. Neither is a hypothesis I hold, only the two that are cheapest to eliminate. This needs no card: it is a read of the census rows the media session is committing under docs/figures alongside per-slice quantities already in the sessions. Spec-level guided; diagnosis only, and its successor decides whether there is anything to repair.

Give the cold first iterate a shadow by reading twice, because the frames it leaves wrong are the ones the consumer depends on most

The wall-anchor repair deliberately excludes the first iterate of a run: it has no previous shadow, and rather than derive one circularly from the boundary it is choosing, it keeps the unmasked read and documents the choice. That was right for that node and it leaves the worst frames unfixed. On the corpus census limited cold-start frames sit at a 0.066 median span ratio against 0.285 for warm ones, over 1,228 frames with 844 of 943 shots contributing one.

The consumer's observation is what promotes this from a leftover to the highest-value remainder, and neither side would have assembled it alone. Its decoder is seeded at the starts of sequences. In training that overlaps the early formation phase already predicted to be worst; in the demo, a base change re-seeds and the first frames after it carry exactly that slice's conditioning, which is the only regime its measurement supports at all. So the frames this repair leaves worst are precisely the ones its short-horizon case depends on most. No magnitude is claimed for the interaction and it needs measuring against a relabelled shot rather than assuming.

The circularity is narrower than it looks and that is the opening. The axis and the saddles are located independently of the boundary; only the boundary selection consumes them, and only a pinned limited class takes its boundary from the wall. So a first iterate can read twice: a first pass establishing the axis and the qualified saddles and a provisional anchor, then the private-wall partition built from those, then a second selection of the anchor under that mask. Nothing in the second pass depends on the first pass's anchor, which is what makes it a bootstrap rather than a circle. Measure the cost before committing to it, since the first iterate is also the expensive one, and if a full second read is too costly consider deriving the partition from the saddles alone, which are the only input the shadow needs from that read.

Gate on the same contracts as the anchor repair, the twelve positive-current bank rows and the Solovev certificate bit-identical and a pinned diverted read untouched, plus the measure that distinguishes success: the limited cold cell moving off 0.066 toward the warm figure on relabelled data, with the warm cell not regressing. State the added first-iterate cost as a number, because a doubled first read on every shot is a real price and the corpus writer pays it once per run. Depends on the anchor repair, which has landed. Spec-level guided; this is delicate topology code and belongs on the codex lane.

Count how many wall nodes the private-wall mask actually excludes per frame, because zero and nonzero send the repair to different places

The wall-anchor masking change is bit-identical to the unrepaired writer on every limited frame of seven relabelled shots, while the diverted control moves at round-off. So the mask is either never firing or firing where it cannot matter, and one census separates those. Do not propose a repair in this node. The last two proposals on this defect, a centre-column exclusion and a new reachability rule, were both refuted by measurement after being argued from the source, and the machinery that was eventually found already existed and was wired the wrong way round.

Deliver, per frame, over the limited frames of the seven shots at /work/projects/imas_gpu/sophelio/labeller_sessions/boundary-repair-validation-20260907T1124Z: the number of wall nodes the mask excluded, its denominator of wall nodes, the number of qualified saddles the predicate consumed, which eligibility branch it took on each side, and whether the node that won the anchor was among the masked ones. That last field is the one that distinguishes the two outcomes. A zero masked count says the predicate never classifies anything on these frames and the predicate is the subject; a nonzero count with a bit-identical output says the masked nodes were not the winning node and the selection is downstream of where the mask applies.

The instrument already exists and is not wired to this question. wall_height_shadow_mask in nova/equilibrium/connectivity_boundary.py takes a report_eligibility keyword that already computes and prints per-side removed-candidate counts and reference separations through a debug callback. Reuse it rather than writing new instrumentation, and if it does not carry a field the census needs, extend it there rather than building a parallel path. Read the predicate before running anything: its height band is derived from qualified saddles, and a limited frame is by definition one with no qualified saddle to bind the boundary, so trace what the branch structure does when neither side is valid and state what the mask returns in that case. Whether that reading holds is exactly what the census settles.

Two facts from the consumer to orient the reading, both labelled as such. On 27079 at 30 ms, the frame with the worst measured span, the winning wall node is the upper roof at R 0.565, Z 1.728, which is neither a centre-column node nor obviously in a private flux region. And the worst floating frame still carries a 1.170 mWb axis-to-boundary span against EFIT's 396 mWb median for that shot, so the defect is three orders of magnitude, not a tolerance question.

Default a cold first iterate to the limited class and let it transition out, so the wall anchor is gated before the boundary is chosen

Lead directive, 2026-09-07: assume cold slices are limited on the very first iterate, and let them transition into diverted if the branch permits. This is the repair, not another diagnosis. The wall-anchor masking change is merged and measured to change nothing on the class it targets, and the reason it cannot is structural: the private-wall exclusion band in wall_height_shadow_mask is formed from qualified saddles, admitting a primary and at most one opposite-side saddle, and returns the previous shadow unchanged where neither side is valid. A limited frame is by definition one with no qualified saddle binding its boundary, and a cold start has no previous shadow, so the shadow is empty and stays empty for exactly the frames it was written for.

The directive dissolves that rather than working around it. If limited is the class a cold first iterate starts in rather than a state it must discover, the anchor selection is gated from the first iterate instead of after a class the frame could not reach. The corpus already behaves this way and the assumption is a reading of it rather than a simplification: the first drawn frame is limited on seven of seven relabelled shots, and it is cold on seven of seven, because a cold frame is by construction one whose stored predecessor failed the guard and a sequence admits only guarded frames.

What the repair must not do. Do not filter, drop or reweight frames anywhere; the deliverable is correct frames, not a smaller set of them. Do not exclude the centre column or any structure by name. Do not set a tolerance from the values currently observed. And do not restate the diagnosis: the mechanism above is a reading of the source and the concurrent census node is measuring whether the mask fires at all, so if that census returns a nonzero masked-node count, say so and reconcile before proceeding, because a firing mask would mean the class default is not where the defect lives.

Measure it on the frames that show it, not on the class as a whole. The visible defect is enclosed area rather than wall standoff, and the standoff figure is the misleading one: a diverted boundary correctly stands a 103.7 mm median off the wall, so a standoff threshold fires on 79 of 81 frames of 27079 including all 53 diverted ones. On area against the reconstruction the two populations separate cleanly, limited at a 0.773 median and 0.042 minimum against diverted at 1.036 and 0.693, with only five frames of eighty-one below half and all five limited and in the first three drawn. The acceptance measure is those frames recovering: the first drawn frame of 27079, at t=30 ms and 4.2 per cent of reference area with 15 of 1324 hex cells, and the two behind it at 33.5 and 57.1 per cent. The 142.91 mm limited standoff median against a limiter the reconstruction touches at 1.1 mm remains a true statement of the physical defect and is the target for the class, but it is not what a viewer sees and must not be the headline.

Gate: the twelve positive-current bank rows and the Solovev certificate bit-identical, a pinned diverted read untouched, and the recovery stated as an area ratio per frame on 27079's first three drawn frames with the frame count printed. Then relabel 27079 into a new root and hand the path to the media session, since a repair that does not reach a regenerated figure has not been delivered. Spec-level guided; delicate topology code, so the codex lane as the lead directed for topology work.

The label reader prefers a stored boundary when present and silently falls back when absent, so it draws different quantities from different corpora

Nova's own label reader carries a prefer-if-present branch on the boundary, and it has already fired. When lcfs_r carries a zero-length vertex dimension it takes the outermost nested flux surface instead, which is documented; when the writer populates the polyline it takes that. The writer began populating it somewhere between the banked corpus and the relabelled one, so every figure drawn from the two roots today drew a different quantity, agreeing to 1e-12 where both exist and therefore invisible. Only the receipts distinguished the outputs, because they report the boundary source from the per-frame count rather than from a constant, and that is the one piece of provenance discipline in the chain that held.

The hazard is the granularity, not the fallback. A fallback keyed on emptiness switches per file and per frame rather than per corpus, so a partially relabelled corpus would mix boundary sources inside one training set, and a consumer would see a provenance change it could not distinguish from physics. The consumer checked its own tree against this and is currently safe by construction: its proximity and conditioning readers take flux_surface_psi_norm, flux_surface_r and flux_surface_z and select the unit normalised-flux level from the nested set, and the only reader of lcfs_r on its side is a referee path against a different data source. So the raster is built from the flux surfaces directly, as the locked raster decision intended. That safety is a property of today's call sites and nothing enforces it.

Two things to settle. First, decide the reader's contract explicitly rather than by fallback: either the stored polyline is authoritative whenever present and a session lacking one is refused, or the nested surface is authoritative always and the stored polyline is never read for a boundary. Say which, and make the unchosen path raise rather than substitute. Second, establish why the writer started persisting a boundary, since it was in no node's declared scope: if it was intended repair work then the repair reached the persistence layer and stopped short of the selection, which localises where to look, and if it was incidental then the relabelled root differs from the banked one in a way nobody planned.

Gate: a test that a session with an empty boundary variable raises rather than falling back once the contract is chosen, a test that a populated one is used, and a stated verdict naming which of the two contracts was taken and why. Also add the source field to any figure or measurement receipt that lacks it, since the receipts are what made this visible at all. Spec-level exact; local lane.

/reckon-ship playable-forward-solve §8

Landed by pfs-label-reader-contract (commit da945ecc, merged 783a6cf7): the stored LCFS polyline is the sole boundary authority in nova/media/sources/nova_labels.py, a frame with an empty lcfs_r raises naming the contract, a session mixing stored and empty frames is refused, and boundary_source is a constant naming the contract; the writer began persisting the polyline when the empty-LCFS fill landed, which is why the stopped corpus root splits 77 populated against 1,024 empty.
